Two months later, her anxiety remains. Her marriage is still strained. But she’s already signed up for another weekend workshop.

You sit in a room. Across from you, a stranger stares through you and whispers, “Stand where your grandmother would.” You obey. Within ten seconds, you’re crying—though you never met her. This is Bert Hellinger’s family constellations therapy. A method that, in the Russian-speaking world, has ballooned into a mass industry. The numbers: One of the largest industry portals reports nearly a thousand certified practitioners in Russia, Ukraine, Belarus, and Kazakhstan—and that’s just the registered ones. The unregistered? Far more. The actual number of facilitators dwarfing official counts. Moscow. St. Petersburg. Yekaterinburg. Baku. Almaty. Thousands of workshops yearly. The market is valued at billions of rubles annually. And this is only the beginning.

The people behind it aren’t outright frauds. Many genuinely believe in what they’re doing. They’ve completed expensive training programs. They read books. They speak at conferences. They see themselves as helping professionals.

And yet the entire industry grew from the biography of a single man—a man whose life and worldview deserve closer examination.

Anton Hellinger. That was his name at birth. 1925. Leimen, southwestern Germany. A deeply Catholic family. Anton, named after Saint Anthony of Padua. Faith became his secret shield. Later, he would claim that his Catholic upbringing protected him from the plague of National Socialism. But how does one resist when ideology bears down on young souls? He refuses to join the Hitler Youth. Risks his life—attends underground Catholic gatherings. And lands on the radar. The Gestapo takes notice. 1942. Seventeen years old. Conscripted into the Wehrmacht. Sent to the Western Front, straight into the slaughter. He survives. But in 1945, at twenty—capture. Belgium. A POW camp, barbed wire, hopelessness. Yet Hellinger doesn’t surrender. He escapes. Makes his way through occupied Europe and returns to Germany.

After the war, Anton joined a Catholic order. Took holy vows. For sixteen years, he lived in South Africa. The backcountry—the rural districts of KwaZulu-Natal. The Zulu people. What kept him there for half his life? The answer lay in blood and soil. Hellinger later claimed he was struck by how traditional African societies pulsed with clan ties, how ancestral reverence permeated everything, how unshakable the hierarchy of lineage was. These were more than customs—they were systems in which the dead gripped the living by the throat, refusing to let them stray. These very themes—the invisible chains of generations—became the core of his method. The revelation was close.

In the 1970s, Hellinger returned to Europe, left the order, and married. He changed his name from Anton to Bert—a childhood nickname. He began studying psychotherapy, trained in group dynamics, Gestalt therapy, transactional analysis, and family therapy. He was particularly influenced by Virginia Satir and Ivan Boszormenyi-Nagy—both pioneering family therapists of the mid-20th century. Boszormenyi-Nagy, a Hungarian-American psychiatrist, developed the concepts of “generational ledgers” and “contextual therapy,” emphasizing the weight of family debts and loyalties passed down through generations. This was, in fact, a crucial—and largely scientifically grounded—field of family therapy.

By the early 1990s, Hellinger had developed his own method. He began holding seminars in Germany. The method was called Familienstellen—“family constellations.” It quickly gained traction. By the late 1990s, Hellinger was conducting trainings worldwide. In the early 2000s, mass workshops took off in Russia, Brazil, Mexico, and South Africa. His books were translated into dozens of languages. He became a cult figure.

What are family constellations – technically speaking?

In Hellinger’s classic version, a group participant steps into the center and asks others to “represent” members of their family. The facilitator arranges these stand-ins intuitively or as instructed by the client. Then, allegedly, the “field” begins to “speak” through them: the representatives feel emotions, utter phrases, move—not as actors playing roles, but supposedly as conduits of information from the client’s actual familial system.

Hellinger claimed families are bound by invisible “orders of love” — Ordnungen der Liebe. But what if this fragile framework cracks? If someone in the lineage was pushed into the shadows? Forgotten. Unmourned. Denied their place, erased from memory, or taken too soon. Hellinger knew the answer: the breach doesn’t vanish. It ruptures through time, embedding itself in the next generations. Symptoms. Illnesses. Failures. An inability to breathe freely or be happy. A family curse? No, Hellinger insisted. A systemic glitch. The constellation’s task is to untangle this dead knot. Restore order. Find the excluded. Bow before them. Acknowledge. Return them to their place. And then, according to Hellinger, the family secret would unravel: “the energy of love” would flow correctly again, and the client would experience healing.

On paper, it sounds beautiful. Even moving. The idea that we’re tied to our ancestors—that forgotten family tragedies shape our lives—has deep roots in human culture. Ancient peoples knew this. Ancestral remembrance rituals are integral to nearly all traditional societies.

But the key question about family constellations isn’t “Is this a beautiful idea?” The key question is: “Does it work? Is there proof?”

This question has a simple, unambiguous answer.

Family constellations. Zero randomized controlled trials. Not one. The entire body of medical literature is a barren wasteland: no published studies in major peer-reviewed journals demonstrate the method outperforms placebo for any specific disorder. Zilch. So why do thousands still flock to it blindly? A glance at the alternative front—a handful of dissertations and small-scale studies. Evidence at last? Hardly. Who conducts these inquiries? Proponents of the method themselves. Where are they published? In niche family constellation journals. A closed loop. And when you strip away the scientific veneer, a shocking truth is laid bare: the methodology of these studies crumbles under even the most basic scrutiny of evidence-based medicine. The effect is revelatory—beneath a thin layer of jargon, there’s nothing but empty space.

The German Society for Systemic Constellations (Deutsche Gesellschaft für Systemische Aufstellungen) — a professional organization in Germany — publicly distanced itself from Hellinger as early as the beginning of the 2000s. In 2004, nearly two hundred certified constellation practitioners signed a declaration dissociating themselves from Hellinger, asserting that he had departed from the fundamental principles of systemic therapy.

Hellinger’s central concept — the “knowing field” (wissendes Feld), which supposedly transmits information from the client’s actual family through the representatives — has no confirmation in physics, biology, or psychology. No physical mechanism explains how strangers standing in the middle of a room can “perceive” information about a person’s family that they are seeing for the first time in their lives.

What actually happens in constellations? The scientific answer is ruthless in its simplicity: suggestion, role-play, emotional expression. A participant steps into the center. Their body is tense, pulse racing. They have already slipped into a hypnotic state — a paralyzing anticipation of revelation. And in this trance, they choose “representatives” by no means at random. Hidden associations, outward resemblance. The brain imperceptibly reads and projects images. But who is directing this performance? The “representatives” receive cues. Implicit, almost imperceptible signals from the facilitator. Barely noticeable gestures from the client themselves. And there they go — playing fiercely, wrenchingly, wringing every last drop of emotion out of their roles. Sometimes this ecstasy helps the client experience something — like catharsis in theater or art therapy. Sometimes it doesn’t. Nothing supernatural is happening here. No mystical field. There is not a single piece of evidence that the effect of constellations exceeds that of an ordinary therapeutic placebo. The illusion is exposed.

Now, on to what is more concerning.

In the early 2000s, Hellinger made a series of public statements that shocked even many of his own students.

On victims and rapists. Hellinger insisted, methodically and mercilessly. A victim of rape or incest must “bow down” to the rapist. Accept him as part of her family system. Forgive. Acknowledge him as her “equal.” Refusal of such “acceptance,” according to Hellinger, blocks healing irreversibly. But how? How do you acknowledge as an equal the one who trampled your will and embodied your worst nightmare? This stands in direct contradiction to everything contemporary trauma literature says. In evidence-based trauma therapy — whether EMDR, prolonged exposure therapy, or cognitive processing therapy — the ironclad first rule is: never force a victim to forgive the rapist. Forgiveness, if it comes at all, is always a late and deeply individual process. Imposing it is not healing. It is re-victimization. A blow struck exactly where the wound is already open.

Hitler. Norway, 2007. Hellinger walks onto the stage — and says what critics will immediately label as apologetics. He has read poems about Hitler. His own poems. In them is a line documented in academic dissertations: Hitler “was no better or worse than any other human being.” Not better. Not worse. Just — like everyone else. Then it goes further. Hellinger speaks of the need to “accept Hitler.” Not to condemn. Not to curse. To accept. As part of the German “family system.” And right on its heels — a comparison that chills the blood: just as a r••• victim must accept her rapist. One phrase. One image. And a whole abyss of questions. What lies behind these words — a philosophy of reconciliation or a justification of evil? Where is the line between acceptance and forgiveness? And who has the right to draw it?

On homosexuality. In a number of public appearances, Hellinger claimed that homosexuality is a “systemic disorder,” usually connected to a person occupying the place of a forgotten deceased relative within the family. Contemporary scientific psychiatry classifies homosexuality not as a disorder but as a variant of the norm (the diagnosis was removed from the DSM as early as 1973 and from ICD-10 in 1992). The notion that homosexuality can be “cured” through constellation work is a form of conversion therapy, which is banned or condemned in most developed countries as harmful pseudomedicine.

On incest. In several works, Hellinger claimed that incest sometimes serves a “systemic function” within the family — for instance, preserving “order” or “compensating” for an absent man. This is, to put it mildly, a dubious interpretation. To put it bluntly — it is a dangerous idea that potentially justifies the sexual abuse of children as a systemic “necessity.”

On women. Hellinger systematically asserted that in a “properly” ordered family, a woman follows the man; that her place is “below” the man; that violating this “order” leads to family misfortune. This is patriarchal ideology, not psychotherapy.

It must be said honestly: not all practitioners of family constellations share these views. Many, especially in Russia and the West, work with gentler, modified versions of the method. Many do not repeat Hellinger’s openly misogynistic or homophobic statements. Many insist: “We use the technique, not the ideology.”

But here an important question arises. If the technique is separable from its author’s ideology, what, exactly, remains as its technical core? Arranging figures in space and reading their “responses”? Yet this device is used in classical family psychotherapy (Virginia Satir, Minuchin’s structural family therapy) without the mystical overlay. These evidence-based methods require no belief in a “knowing field” or “orders of love.” They work through recognized psychological mechanisms: visualization, role-play, communication training, systemic reframing.

When Hellinger’s students claim they preserve the “technique” while rejecting his “ideology,” what they’re often preserving is the mystical core of his method, while discarding his most publicly objectionable statements. Yet, it’s this mystical core that turns constellation work into pseudoscience. Remove it, and what remains is simply a role-playing exercise—hardly something worthy of charging forty-five thousand rubles for a weekend session.

The professional evaluation of family constellation therapy in contemporary scientific literature boils down to several key points.

First, the method fails to meet basic criteria for psychotherapy: there’s no structured diagnostic process, no clear contractual agreement, no supervision, no confidentiality standards (constellations are conducted publicly, in a group of strangers), no alternative methods when the approach proves ineffective, and no mechanism for informed consent.

Second, the method is dangerous for individuals with serious mental disorders. Participants with schizophrenia, bipolar disorder, severe depression, or PTSD experience intense emotional stimulation without any psychiatric support. Documented cases include acute decompensations following constellation sessions, including psychotic episodes and, in rare instances, suicidal attempts.

Third, the method violates the fundamental ethical code of psychotherapy. The facilitator puts on a quasi-therapeutic mask without possessing any actual qualifications as a psychotherapist. What do they do? They expose the client’s most intimate family secrets. And to whom? To a group of strangers. Outsiders stare bluntly at raw, uncovered wounds. But that’s not all. They pass judgment. They issue direct commands: “Bow to your mother!” “Forgive your father!” “Leave your former partner!” On what are these harsh verdicts based? Solely on subjective “field perceptions.” None of these practices has the slightest chance of surviving within evidence-based psychotherapy.

Fourth, the method’s commercial success stems precisely from its ineffectiveness. A paradox? No, just the cold logic of the market. Imagine if a single constellation session solved the problem—the client would leave forever, disappearing into obscurity. But it doesn’t solve anything. So the client returns. Again and again. Why? “Apparently, I need to work more on my family system,” whispers the inner voice. “Apparently, there are forgotten ancestors.” “Apparently, I need to do a constellation with other representatives.” Every phrase is a new hook. Every doubt a new twist. Participation turns into a years-long dependence on the facilitator. And dependence becomes a steady, inexhaustible source of income for them.

Finally, and most painfully, there are the consequences for psychiatric patients.

In my practice, they come back repeatedly. People who have attended constellation sessions for years. A thirty-five-year-old woman. Early signs of bipolar disorder. For three consecutive years, she attended workshops. For three years, they “worked on her lineage.” For three years, she was told: “You need to accept your ancestors. You need to forgive your mother. You need to take your place in the system.” But what if the symptoms aren’t a “call of the blood”? Periodic hypomanias with the risk of escalating into mania. Depressive episodes. Sleep disturbances. All of this was mercilessly interpreted as “ancestral messages.” For three years, her true illness was masked as mysticism. When she finally crossed the threshold of a psychiatric office, it was a full-blown manic episode. No insight. A real risk to her job. A real threat to her family. She heard her diagnosis for the first time in her life.

She’s thirty-five. Three years ago, she could have been prescribed lithium or another mood stabilizer to regulate her condition, allowing her to live and work normally. Instead, she spent all this time being told about “family constellations.” She left my office with a prescription—and the bitter realization that three years of her life had been stolen.

Bert Hellinger died in 2019 at ninety-three. His commercial empire lives on. His method continues to be marketed—especially aggressively in Russian-speaking regions, where weak regulation of psychological services makes it a lucrative industry.

Every time I see an ad for yet another “Hellinger Constellations—Transform Your Family Legacy in One Weekend!” workshop, I think of that patient. Of her three lost years. Of all the others like her who haven’t made it to my office yet. And of those who never will.

I’m writing this chapter not to offend any particular practitioner. Most are true believers—people who paid dearly for their training, convinced they’re helping others. I’m writing this for patients and their families. For those facing a choice: where to turn with psychological distress, anxiety, depression, or family struggles.

If you’re reading this—stop. Don’t go to a constellations facilitator. It’s a trap. Go to a clinical psychologist. To someone whose training is backed by evidence. Go to a psychotherapist practicing cognitive-behavioral, systemic-family, psychodynamic, or another science-based modality. Unsure about the professional you’re about to see? Vet them. Ask to see their state-certified diploma, professional association membership, publication record. A real specialist will hand you the documents—calmly, without offense or evasion. A pseudo-specialist will balk. Their face will twitch. They’ll redirect the conversation, steer you away from the point. Fear that shadow.

Constellations won’t “heal your family legacy.” Such a thing doesn’t exist. What does exist is your mind, your body, your family, your present environment. These can be worked with. A “family legacy” can’t. Because it isn’t real.

RED FLAG. If a practitioner blames your current symptoms on your ancestors—“generational trauma,” “systemic entanglements,” “unresolved family history”—you’re not in therapy. Your struggles aren’t buried in dead people’s biographies. Walk out.

24 / 36

Chapter 20. ThetaHealing: The Revelation of Vianna Stibal

1995. A tiny American town called Idaho Falls, Idaho. Forty-year-old Vianna Stibal walked into a doctor’s office. The pain — stubborn, dragging, lodged deep in her right hip — had dug in and wouldn’t let go. To cut or not to cut? The needle went into the flesh. The biopsy was done. Years passed. And then forensic investigators, digging through Vianna’s medical records, stumbled on a single word. A key. One that changed everything. “Suspicion.” The biopsy results pointed to a malignant process, but they remained inconclusive. Non-diagnostic. A shadow on the scan. A ghost of an illness. Not a single doctor on the planet gave Vianna Stibal a confirmed diagnosis of cancer. No one prescribed chemotherapy. No one proposed amputating her leg.

This would be established in 2016 by the Idaho Supreme Court, based on the expert assessment of an independent specialist, Dr. Christian Schall, the testimony of her ex-husband given under oath, and an analysis of the medical documents themselves entered into the case file.

But in 1995, according to Vianna Stibal’s own account, she was diagnosed with “bone cancer of the femur.” She was supposedly told that without chemotherapy or amputation, she would die. Stibal — a naturopath with no medical training — refused standard treatment and instead turned to prayer and meditation. By her claim, she healed within minutes — a tumor “nine inches” (about twenty-three centimeters) across vanished the moment her “brainwave activity shifted into the theta range” and she “established a direct connection with the Almighty Creator of All That Is.”

From this story grew a global industry called ThetaHealing. Or, officially, ThetaHealing® — a trademark registered in the United States in 2006.

Here’s what lies behind the words “meditation training technique.” Behind the “spiritual philosophy” for “mind, body, and spirit.” Behind the attractive signboard on Vianna Stibal’s official website. Theta waves. A state of deep relaxation. That, Stibal claims, is where the practitioner must sink their brain. And then — here’s the revelation — a “direct connection to the Creator” becomes possible. And it gets better. In this state, she says, you can instantly change beliefs at the level of the client’s DNA. Erase “limiting programs.” Heal physical illnesses. Clear “ancestral programs.” But that’s not all. At one of her British lectures, she made a claim that must have left the audience frozen. Regrowing amputated limbs. Restoring removed ovaries. Not a metaphor. Not an allegory. Literally. Just like that: relax, enter the theta waves, connect to the Creator — and the arm is back. The ovary is working again. Beliefs rewritten right in the spirals of DNA. Vianna Stibal calls this meditation.

Theta waves. Frequency 4–8 Hz. They’re recorded by an electroencephalograph — that’s a fact. During sleep. In deep meditation. Under intense emotional arousal. All of it confirmed. All of it measurable. But Stibal goes further. Through the theta rhythm, she claims, a person establishes a direct connection with the “Creator of All That Is.” And this connection rewrites the client’s DNA. Stop. DNA? Genetics is an exact science. Neuroscience is too. Neither of them knows a single mechanism by which the theta rhythm could alter the genetic code. Not one experiment. Not one publication. Not one line of data. A real rhythm. Real 4–8 Hz. Real sleep. Real meditation. And the conclusion — emptiness. A scientific-looking wrapper around a myth. And the client believes it: the numbers are real, after all.

To the “theta waves” were added references to quantum mechanics (a common trick of modern pseudoscience), to the law of attraction, to epigenetics. All these words sound impressive. All of them are torn from their scientific context and applied to claims they have nothing to do with.

Stibal’s empire is not a small one. Remember that. The ThetaHealing Institute of Knowledge. Abbreviated — THInK. Registered in Idaho Falls. A single legal address — and the whole world thrown in. Courses. Certified “theta healers.” Dozens of countries: Russia, Ukraine, Kazakhstan, Azerbaijan. The list is long. And now — how it all works. The basic course. Want to practice — pay. Want to teach others — pay more. Advanced level. Want to teach at the upper tiers — pay even more. A pyramid. And at its very apex — one woman. Stibal herself. She regularly “channels new information from the Creator” and sells access to that information through seminars. Every new level, every new training, every new portion of “information from above” — with a price tag. The question is simple. Who actually pays for the revelations — the Creator or the students?

In 2008 Stibal made a move that turned out to be fatal for her. Her institute began offering a “doctorate” in ThetaHealing. This degree was advertised as a “PhD,” “Doctorate of Ministry,” or simply “Doctorate.” Students paid substantial sums for the additional courses required to obtain this diploma.

Kara Alexander. A young American. No fool. No casual dabbler. She paid real money for the “doctorate.” Behind her — a bachelor’s degree with a double major: art and biology. A medical illustrator. Plus a partially completed master’s in neuroscience. And then she dropped New York. Moved to Idaho Falls. For what? For one person. For Stibal. And she believed. Sincerely. In the story about being healed of cancer.

In the fall of 2008, Alexander and other students began asking questions about the legitimacy of the “doctorate.” It emerged that no ThetaHealing doctorate exists in any state registry of educational institutions. That the diploma Alexander received was just a plaque on the wall.

Alexander filed a lawsuit. Her claim contained three main points: fraud, breach of contract, and a demand for punitive damages.

Between 2013 and 2015, the Idaho courts heard the case of Alexander v. Stibal. Word for word. Page by page. Stibal’s own medical records—the ones pertaining to her alleged 1995 “hip cancer”—landed on the judge’s desk. And then came the bombshell no one saw coming. The expert testimony of Dr. Christian Schall delivered a verdict of its own: the biopsy results in those records showed only suspicion of malignancy. Suspicion. Not a diagnosis. Never—not once—a confirmed diagnosis. No doctor. No document. Never in Stibal’s entire medical history was there a confirmed cancer diagnosis. And now—the worst of it. The recommendation for amputation or chemotherapy. The very story she’d told publicly. From grand stages. In countless speeches. It appears in zero medical records. Not one signature. Not one line. Silence.

Stibal’s ex-husband also testified under oath: he confirmed that in 1995, his wife was never definitively told she had cancer.

Alexander won the bulk of her case. The jury awarded her $111,000 in contract damages, $17,000 for fraud, and $500,000 in punitive damages. The latter was later reduced by the district court to $384,000, in line with Idaho law.

2016. The Idaho Supreme Court. Case files open to the public—Alexander v. Stibal, 160 Idaho 10, 368 P.3d 630. Behind the dry legal language lies a condemnation of deceit. The court partially reversed the lower court’s ruling on the contract claim. But for fraud and punitive damages? The hammer fell. Ironclad affirmation. Turn to the reasoning. What was Stibal’s claim? That she had healed herself from cancer using ThetaHealing. Pause. Cancer. Healing. But the judicial machine doesn’t take claims on faith. The person who swore they’d beaten cr with the power of thought? Now trapped in the snare of truth. The court states plainly: this was a knowingly false assertion. Fraudulent misrepresentation. Proven? Yes. How? By clear and convincing evidence. No gray areas. The mask is off.

The court further noted that Stibal “aggressively marketed” her courses using her cancer healing story; that this story played a key role in Alexander’s decision to pay substantial sums for training; and that THInK’s commercial atmosphere justified punitive damages.

Stibal’s public response appears on her own website. There, she claims the court “just wanted proof of the word ‘diagnosis’”; that the ruling was “biased against her on religious grounds”; that she “won 90% of the case”; that “ThetaHealing has never faced criminal fraud charges.” (Technically true—it was civil—but irrelevant: the court found fraud.)

How do independent scientific experts assess ThetaHealing?

McGill University’s Office for Science and Society — a leading science communication center in Canada — classifies ThetaHealing as pseudoscience. In an article on the office’s website, ThetaHealing is described with the word “woo” — a term used in the scientific skeptical community to denote mystical practices masquerading as science.

Edzard Ernst knows what he’s talking about. A German-born Briton, a physician, the founder of the world’s first university chair in complementary medicine at the University of Exeter. Author of more than a thousand scientific publications. He has spent his life studying alternative treatments. Not a theorist. Not a dilettante. A man who for decades has been exposing the emptiness beneath the shell of “miracle cures.” And this man takes up ThetaHealing. And utters one word. Criminal. Not “questionable.” Not “unproven.” Not “controversial.” Criminal. Why? Because behind the facade of the method lies the sale of physically impossible “healings.” To whom? To seriously ill people. To those clinging to a last straw. To those ready to give everything. And Ernst delivers his verdict: this is not merely unethical behavior. It is a criminally punishable act. In any adequate jurisdiction.

RationalWiki, an independent scientific skeptical resource, makes a simple calculation: by Stibal’s own account, she has “taken on about forty thousand clients,” and her certified practitioners — thousands more. If the method worked at least at the level of a placebo effect, there would be thousands of documented cases of healing from serious illnesses in the public domain. There are none. There are only anecdotal testimonials on the website, unverifiable, and — far more numerous — complaints from people who paid but did not get what was promised.

In Russia, Belarus, Kazakhstan, Ukraine, and Azerbaijan, ThetaHealing is spreading fast. On Russian-language Instagram, tens of thousands of “certified practitioners.” Groups in messaging apps where “theta healers” exchange experience. Coaches combining ThetaHealing with constellation work, regressive therapy, “ancestral tree work,” astrology, and tarot.

The prices are standard: a session runs from five to fifteen thousand rubles an hour. Basic-level training — from forty to eighty thousand for a three-day seminar. Advanced levels — from one hundred to three hundred thousand. A “master class from Vianna Stibal in person” — up to a million rubles, depending on the format.

What does the client actually get? A session. The practitioner sits down across from them. Closes their eyes. Their breathing slows, the muscles of their face relax — they enter the so-called theta state. They supposedly read limiting beliefs. Their lips whisper the ritual phrase: “Creator of All That Is, change belief X to belief Y.” And firmly: repeat. The client obediently repeats. But what is this theta state worth? And can a miracle even be measured? In 2016, scientists decided to find out. A real experiment. Caps with EEG electrodes were placed on the heads of the “healers” — for the appearance of science and as a final test. Cold conductors dug into the skin. The reading of brain activity began. They wait for a surge in theta rhythm. They wait for proof. The result? Devastating. No specific theta state was registered in the practitioners. Zero. Nothing. And what about the clients? Ordinary EEG changes. The same as with any meditation. Or with banal relaxation on a couch. No miracle. Only an illusion.

Now — to what matters most for our book, for this very chapter.

I’m a psychiatrist. I’ve seen patients broken by years of ThetaHealing. Thirty-eight years old. Female. Severe depressive disorder. For two years, she attended a “theta healer” instead of starting antidepressants and therapy. Two years of her life surrendered to darkness. What was she told? “An energy block in the third chakra.” Linked to “trauma from a past life.” We’ll “reformat” it. Sound like a spell? Exactly. And for every word—payment. Fifteen thousand rubles every week. Week after week. Fifteen thousand. A step into the void. She paid for her own undoing. Two years later, this woman crossed the threshold of my office. With s••••••••••• thoughts.

Another case. A mother. A teenager. First psychotic episode. Wasted weeks. She took her son not to a psychiatrist, but to a “theta healer.” Believed he’d just “clear the energy channels.” But the channels never cleared. The illness gnawed at his brain. When she finally brought him to the hospital, it was too late. The psychosis had gained full force. Acute delusions shackled his mind. What an antipsychotic could have resolved in a week with early intervention now took two brutal months of hospitalization. Two long ward-bound weeks. The price of blind hope.

These aren’t isolated incidents. This is systemic. Every day, in hundreds of post-Soviet cities, people lose time, money, and lives by relying on “theta healers” instead of real medicine.

I’m not writing this for Vianna Stibal to realize she’s wrong. She won’t. Her financial stake in preserving the method is too large. I’m writing this for patients and their families. For those at the crossroads: pay for a “session with the Creator of All That Is” or book an appointment with a psychiatrist.

Not a single ThetaHealing session has ever cured a single mental disorder. Not one. Why? Gurus whisper the method “hasn’t fully unfurled.” Masters shake their heads: “the client wasn’t open enough.” Devotees scramble for excuses: “the energy hasn’t arrived yet.” But strip away the noise. Here’s the key: healing requires one thing—what you do must at least intersect with the actual mechanisms behind your disorder’s origin and treatment. A point of entry. A concrete target. A hard physiological link. ThetaHealing isn’t just distant from these mechanisms—it has no relation to them whatsoever. None at all. Not a single verifiable one.

Tumors vanishing during ThetaHealing? A mystery. But every mystery has a rational key. First option: tumors that never existed—like Stibal’s own. Illusion. Mirage. Second option: the tumors shrink only because actual, real medicine is working in parallel. Chemotherapy. The surgeon’s blade. No miracles here. Depression cured in one session? Impressive. But pull the thread—and again, emptiness. These are only mild situational reactions. They dissolve on their own, like morning mist, leaving no mark on the tormented body. And the schizophrenia that “recedes”? Third trick. Either it was never there—just a fatal misdiagnosis by the “healer”—or it’s a natural remission, already dormant in the mind. Healing is credited to what always followed its own rigid biological laws.

A closed ward. Shadows frozen on the walls. On the bed, a young woman. For six months her mother had been taking her to a theta healer. Six months. Instead of aminazine. Six months of lost time. And now — bars on the window. By the glass, a man of forty. Unblinking. Staring at a single point. Silent. For two years he had lived for this: the Creator of All That Exists will change his DNA. Two years. God didn’t come. The DNA stayed the same. And somewhere beyond these walls, a desperate patient with OCD handed over three hundred thousand rubles. Three hundred thousand. And got nothing. Nothing that could have saved her. Why? Because the methods of salvation were not for sale at that price.

This is not selling sausage. For sausage that fails to match its stated specifications, the seller bears liability. For a “connection with the Creator of All That Exists” that matches no reality whatsoever, the seller bears no liability in any jurisdiction of the post-Soviet space.

We will revisit this asymmetry later.

RED FLAG. If someone promises you healing from a diagnosed illness — any illness — through “energy,” “intention,” “DNA restructuring,” or “connection with the Creator,” and especially if they also advise you to “let go” of your medications — you are not talking to a healer. You are talking to the cause of your future relapse. Walk away — and don’t stop your medications.

25 / 36

Chapter 21. The Past-Life Market: Regressology and “Proprietary Methods”

On Instagram, a Russian-speaking specialist bills herself as a “psychologist, educator, founder of her own online school and ‘unique proprietary method,’ host of her own show, and bestselling author” — commanding an audience of 100,000 followers across social media.

More than one hundred thousand people.

For context: the medical journal Psychiatry and Psychopharmacotherapy in the same Russian-speaking sphere has a print circulation of just a few hundred copies. The largest professional association of psychiatrists in Russia counts about 3,000 active members. The top clinical psychology journal has a few thousand subscribers to its website.

A single blog by one person with an uncertified method reaches an audience dozens of times larger than the entirety of Russia’s professional psychiatric community. And this is just one of dozens of such figures in the Russian-speaking world.

This illustrates what’s now called authority inflation in the age of social media—a phenomenon we’ll explore in detail later, in the chapter on the social media pandemic. For now, let’s focus on the specific market segment that produces and sells this authority.

Regression therapy, or “past-life regression,” claims to guide clients into a hypnotic or “trance” state to “recall” past events—often from previous lives. The idea is chillingly enticing: unresolved issues in your current life—unexplained fears, chronic illnesses, relationship struggles, financial failures—are supposedly echoes of traumas carried over from past incarnations. But what if you could delve into that darkness? Remember those traumas, “process” them, and watch them dissolve—finally fixing your present life.

Here’s what really underpins this. A chain spanning millennia: Hindu and Buddhist concepts of reincarnation. 19th-century Franco-Spanish spiritualist traditions—Allan Kardec. Mid-20th-century American New Age. Three streams. One idea. But who turned ancient belief into a Western self-help technique? American hypnotherapist Michael Newton (1931–2016). Author of two books—Journey of Souls and Destiny of Souls—he became the key theorist behind modern regression therapy in its Western form. And here’s the twist. Newton claimed that under regression hypnosis, patients didn’t just describe past lives. They spoke of something else—the “spaces between lives.” There, they said, souls planned their next incarnation, met “spiritual guides,” and stood before “councils of elders.” Past lives were merely the doorway. Beyond it lay the blueprint for the next.

Another major figure was Dolores Cannon (1931–2014), a British-American hypnotherapist who developed the “Quantum Healing Hypnosis Technique” (QHHT).

In the Russian-speaking world, regression therapy spread throughout the 2000s and 2010s as part of a broader movement known as “esoteric psychology.” Gradually it grew into an industry in its own right, complete with its own hierarchy, training centers, and certifying bodies.

What does serious science have to say about regression therapy?

Is reincarnation a myth? Or a scientific reality? The answer lies not in vague prophecies but in rigorous laboratories. For years, scientists searched for physical, biological, genetic, or neural mechanisms that could preserve and transmit information about past lives. Not one. Zero. Nothing. Not a single confirmation. Not a single lead. All that remains for believers in reincarnation is faith. And that is their right. But science says otherwise: the brain, memory, genes — everything we know about them contradicts the idea of souls migrating from one body to another. Reincarnation? It’s a beautiful idea. But it isn’t science.

And this is where the real danger begins. Regression hypnosis is a procedure that scientific psychology has long and officially regarded as a high-risk practice capable of generating false memories. This is not a conjecture or a hypothesis. It is a well-studied phenomenon. Behind it stands a name — Elizabeth Loftus, a professor at the University of California, Irvine. For several decades she conducted one experiment after another, and those experiments became classics. What did she demonstrate? Under directed pressure from a hypnotist, a psychotherapist, or an interrogator, the human brain is capable of generating memories — entirely fabricated ones, events that never happened. And here is the paradox that chills the blood: to the person themselves, these memories are absolutely real. They are not lying, not faking. They genuinely remember.

In the 1980s and early 1990s, North America experienced a veritable epidemic of so-called “recovered memories” of satanic ritual abuse. Thousands of adult women, guided by regression therapists, “recalled” how their parents had participated in satanic rituals during their childhood, sacrificed children, and performed ritual rapes. On the basis of these “memories,” families were torn apart, criminal cases were opened, and innocent parents were sent to prison. Subsequently, a significant portion of these cases was reviewed, convictions were overturned, and the method of “memory recovery” was recognized as a dangerous pseudotherapy. In the 1990s, the American Psychological Association published an official document warning against its use.

If this applies to “recovered memories” of one’s current life, it applies all the more to “memories” of previous lives. Here, even the debate about the possible reality of trauma is meaningless: there simply is no reality.

So what actually happens in a past-life regression session?

The client is sunk in hypnotic relaxation. The body feels heavy, the pulse slows. And then comes a series of leading questions: “Look at your feet — what shoes are you wearing? Is that sand around you, or cobblestone? What are you dressed in — a gown or armor? Are you a man right now, or a woman? What era is this?” The brain, primed by these questions, instantly begins generating images. More often than not, they’re vivid, emotional, frighteningly detailed. What is this? A revelation? Forgotten lives coming back to life? No. It’s called confabulation. The ruthless manufacture of plausible but entirely false memories.

Then the “regressionist” steers these images in the desired direction: “Now look — why do you have this fear of water in your current life? Who is doing this to you? What is he saying?” And the client “remembers” that in a past life they drowned, or were drowned, or drowned someone else. Once the “regression” is over, the regressionist offers an interpretation: “Your present fear of water is tied to that trauma. We’ve worked through it. The fear should now go away.”

Sometimes the fear does go away — for a while. This effect is well known to evidence-based psychotherapists: experiencing a coherent narrative about your fear can bring temporary relief. That’s how placebo works. That’s how confession works. That’s how talking to a friend works. There is nothing specific to regression therapy in that relief.

And then the fear comes back. Because the real mechanisms behind it have never been touched. So the client returns for the next session. “Clearly we need to dig deeper.” “There’s an even earlier trauma, in an even more distant life.” The process can go on for years.

In the Russian-speaking world, the past fifteen years have produced an entire generation of “specialists” whose business is built on this scheme. Several types stand out.

The first type is the relatively modest practitioner, working within a small circle of clients, with no claims to broad fame. Their services run from three to fifteen thousand rubles per session. They rarely step into the public eye.

The second type is the mid-tier blogger, with somewhere between a thousand and a hundred thousand followers on social media. They run Instagram accounts, hold online webinars, sell self-study courses. Prices range from twenty to a hundred thousand rubles per course.

The third type is the major influencer. A hundred thousand followers or more. Events for thousands of people in rented halls. Bestselling books. Their own “schools.” Sometimes a YouTube show or appearances in other media. Incomes in the tens of millions of rubles a year.

A striking representative of the third type is the Russian figure I mentioned earlier. Her public biography, as laid out in her own social media and open sources, goes like this. She began her career as a regressionist — that is, a practitioner of “past-life recall” in the classic sense. Then she moved into a broader range of activities: “Hellinger constellations,” “working with the ancestral program,” proprietary trainings. At some point came a rebranding: dropping direct references to “past lives” and shifting to a “proprietary method.”

She calls her method a “unique proprietary technology.” According to her own publications, the essence of the method is to help the client “become aware of their role in creating their own reality,” “acknowledge the truth about themselves,” and “become the cause of their own life.” She has written a bestselling book about “accepting the causality of one’s life,” and she puts out a weekly author’s show.

At the level of wording, this doesn’t sound so bad. “Acknowledge your role,” “become the cause of your life”—these are statements that could come up in evidence-based psychotherapy (cognitive behavioral therapy really does work with the client’s responsibility for their decisions; acceptance and commitment therapy is even more direct about it). The problem isn’t the wording. The problem is the content of the actual sessions, the methods, the qualifications, the results.

The actual content of this practitioner’s sessions, as shown in her own public appearances and in clips posted online, often involves work with “past lives,” “ancestral programs,” and the “collective unconscious.” The client is told that their current problem is “karma,” an “ancestral script,” an “unfulfilled task of the soul.” Then this gets “worked through” via visualizations, conversations with the “inner child,” and “ancestral forgiveness.” It’s exactly what any regression therapist does—just under a new brand.

The perfect trajectory. The script is calibrated down to the millimeter. Public perception of the method is falling apart. “Hellinger constellations” or “regressive therapy”—these names no longer inspire enthusiasm. They provoke revulsion. Criticism appears in the press. Skeptics bare their teeth. Exposé articles land one after another, hitting the target dead-on. And what do the practitioners do? They change the name. An instant repaint. The “constellation facilitators” peel off the old label and become “systemic-phenomenological therapists.” The “regression therapists” shed their skin and turn into “specialists in method X.” The essence? The method stays the same. Only the packaging changes.

This is the industry’s survival strategy under growing criticism. A similar approach is used in the commercial sphere when a product gets a bad reputation: it’s renamed, redesigned, and its advertising is adjusted. Except in this case, the thing being sold isn’t cookies—it’s psychological services for people in emotional pain.

What’s needed here is absolute clarity. I am not filing a lawsuit against this practitioner. I am not bringing criminal charges. But let’s look at the facts. Her entire chain of techniques—posted in open sources, available to anyone—has no scientific basis. Zero. Nothing. So why do thousands of people hand her money? The answer is harsh: her business model hits the target dead-on. She coldly exploits psychological vulnerability. People get hooked by their own fears and pain. This is my professional assessment. And it isn’t forged from rumors. Its foundation is material open to the whole world. But imagine: one day this practitioner makes a different choice. She discards the mysticism of “past lives.” She cuts away the occult “ancestral programs.” She moves toward evidence-based psychology—toward scientific truth. I’ll be the first to celebrate.

The problem isn’t any single individual. The problem is the industry itself. The hundreds or thousands of people operating under the same schemes. The lack of regulation. The fact that someone with an accredited university degree in psychology is legally equivalent to someone who took a three-day paid course in “Method X”—both equally entitled to call themselves “psychologists” and charge money for “consultations.”

Let me share one specific clinical case. Details have been altered for anonymity, but the essence remains unchanged.

A thirty-eight-year-old woman came to see me—we’ll call her Aygün. She had textbook anxiety disorder with panic attacks. The first episode occurred at thirty-two, after her divorce. Then it escalated: panic attacks, avoidance of public transport, agoraphobia, insomnia, nausea.

Six years before meeting me, Aygün had avoided psychiatrists—she “didn’t want to be on medication,” as she put it. A friend recommended a “great regression therapist.” Price: eight thousand rubles per session. Over two years, Aygün underwent about forty-five sessions. Total cost: roughly three hundred sixty thousand rubles.

During these sessions, Aygün “recalled” no fewer than six “past lives.” She had been a French peasant in the 14th century, a Middle Eastern merchant, a drowned river girl, and a warrior slain in battle. Each ended in a gruesome death. The regression specialist linked every “memory” to her current symptoms. The pieces formed a terrifying puzzle. “This is why you fear going outside—you were murdered there in a past life.” One sentence—and her brain flinched with icy recognition. “These choking sensations? You drowned.” Phantom water flooded her throat. But how? Did her body really remember? Or was someone feeding fresh trauma to her fear?

The panic attacks didn’t stop. Each session brought a few days of “relief”—then the wave would crash over her again. The therapist would say, “You haven’t reached the deepest layer yet. We must continue.”

By the time Aygün came to me, she was exhausted. Clinically: anxiety disorder with untreated panic attacks. No schizophrenia. No past lives. A standard condition, highly treatable with cognitive behavioral therapy—or SSRIs in severe cases.

We started treatment. Significant improvement after six months. Near-total remission within a year. Cost: the standard price of real treatment, roughly one-tenth what she’d paid the regression therapist.

Those three hundred sixty thousand rubles are gone forever. So are those six years of her life.

And this brings me back to the core idea of this book.

Aigun is not insane. Aigun is an ordinary person. An intelligent woman with a university degree, a job, a family. She was looking for help. Someone recommended it to her. She went.

Is the friend who recommended it insane? No. She is a kind friend who had a similar experience herself, and she sincerely wanted to help.

Is the regression therapist insane? An interesting question. Formally, no — he has no psychiatric diagnosis. But what he says in earnest — earnestly claims to remember his clients’ past lives, earnestly links their present symptoms to fourteenth-century France — is closer in content to psychotic experience than to scientific practice. Only he isn’t suffering. He’s earning.

Here is the question that jolts through every page of this book: which of them is insane? One is in the acute ward. Diagnosis: schizophrenia. He whispers to the doctors that he hears the voice of his ancestor. The response is instant: antipsychotics. An injection. His world rapidly contracts to four hospital walls. The other is in the very center of Moscow. Openly declares: “I channel the voices of my clients’ past lives.” A hundred thousand eyes eagerly follow him on social media. His fee per session is eight thousand rubles. Psychiatric hospitalization — never. Diagnosis — never. Disciplinary action — never. One hears the voice of an ancestor — and gets an injection. The other hears the voices of strangers’ past lives — and gets paid. Who here is in a state of insanity?

This is not a rhetorical question. It is a question for which the modern world, in the midst of a pandemic of madness, has no legal answer. And it should.

RED FLAG. If the cause of your problems is being sought in “past lives” or in “repressed memories” that you are invited to “recall” under hypnosis — know this: the science of memory has shown that such methods do not retrieve memories; they create them. You will leave the session with someone else’s biography and your same old diagnosis. Leave earlier.

26 / 36

Chapter 22. Hubbard and Dianetics: Merchants of Manufactured Happiness

On September 3, 1950, the New York Herald Tribune Book Review published a critique of a book that its own author claimed represented “a cornerstone for Man comparable to the discovery of fire and surpassing in importance the inventions of the wheel and the bow.”

Thirty-nine years old. American. Lafayette Ronald Hubbard. He preferred to go by his initials—L. Ron Hubbard (1911–1986). Former merchant marine sailor. A moderately successful science fiction writer, author of hundreds of short stories and novellas across genres: sci-fi, adventure, Westerns. Now this man was claiming something entirely different. The book was titled Dianetics: The Modern Science of Mental Health. What if the single root of all evil had been found? Hubbard declared that he had done precisely that—uncovered the singular source. Of all neuroses. Psychoses. Criminal tendencies. Psychosomatic illnesses. One origin point for infinite human suffering—and now exposed. But the real revelation was yet to come. The grand unveiling: Hubbard claimed to possess a method that could completely cure every disorder. Absolutely every one.

The man assigned to review this book was no ordinary critic. Erich Fromm. 1900–1980. By then, one of the most prominent names in global psychoanalysis. A German Jew. In 1934, he left Germany for good—fleeing the Nazi regime. One of the founders of neo-Freudianism, a movement merging Freud’s ideas with social and cultural theory. Author of already-published works: Escape from Freedom (1941), Man for Himself (1947). His expertise in psychoanalysis? Virtually unrivaled. His authority in mental health? At the time of publication—near absolute. And so this man—with such a name, such a background, such weight—sat down to write his review.

Fromm’s critique of Dianetics was bluntly titled: “For Seekers of Instant Happiness.”

Fromm began by outlining the state of society. Never before, he wrote, had people been so intensely interested in psychology and the art of living as in the mid-20th century. After the horrors of world war, the collapse of long-held values, and the disillusionment with ideologies, millions were searching for answers—about life’s meaning, personal happiness, how to build relationships, how to better themselves. This was natural. Even healthy.

But among the books catering to this need, he identified two distinct categories. Some were wise, serious, grounded in real knowledge—helping readers work on themselves with the understanding that true growth requires time, effort, and brutal honesty. Others pandered to laziness, promising fabricated happiness cheaply and quickly. Dianetics, Fromm wrote, belonged squarely to the latter. Its author, Hubbard, “employs all the clichés of the success formula with a surprising lack of embarrassment.”

What exactly does Hubbard promise? Everything. Absolutely everything. Any mental disorder. Any psychosomatic pain. He claims to have uncovered the root. The single cause of all ailments at once. He calls them “engrams.” Invisible scars. Imprints of past nightmares. They lie trapped in the “reactive mind,” paralyzing the “analytical mind.” Consider this: one mechanism—responsible for psychosis, stomach ulcers, and migraines alike. One cause—for the entire catalog of human afflictions. But with his technique, he says, if you burn away, “clear” the flesh and mind of these engrams—the chain is broken. Before you stands a Clear. Clear. A person freed from sickness. A person who has forgotten neurotic reactions. Intelligence—above average. Memory—flawless. Mental health—impeccable. A perfect machine. Perfect control.

Hubbard’s book, Fromm wrote, draws on “philosophical foundations” of diverse origins: ancient Hindus, early Greeks and Romans (including Lucretius), the works of Francis Bacon, Darwin’s research, and scattered thoughts of Herbert Spencer. But, Fromm continued, the genuine connection between Dianetics and these teachings is close to zero. Dianetics “undoubtedly does not derive from these doctrines.”

Instead, Fromm concluded, “Dianetics” represents something entirely different: a poorly understood and inverted Freudianism, spiced up with new terminology and regression experiments from the era of hypnotism. The human being in Dianetics is not the complex, multidimensional, freedom-seeking entity described in the great Eastern and Western traditions. In Hubbard’s view, man is a machine, a mechanism, an engineered system. Working with him comes down to pressing buttons. “In such an engineering science as Dianetics, we work by pressing buttons,” Hubbard himself described his method. You don’t need to know or understand anything; just apply Hubbard’s theory of engrams.

Fromm delivered a harsh verdict. “Hubbard’s book,” he wrote, “is difficult to seriously consider as a contribution to the science of man. However, it must be taken with full seriousness as a symptom of a dangerous tendency. If it were merely a highly simplified popularization of Freud’s early theories, it would be harmless. But ‘Dianetics’ is an expression of a spirit diametrically opposed to Freud’s teachings.”

Fromm’s review is a document from 1950. At that time, Hubbard was just one of many popular authors in the “self-help” genre. No one yet foresaw what his movement would become a decade later.

1952. Hubbard makes his move. Dianetics is just a sketch. Now they take center stage—“spiritual beings.” Thetans. An endless cycle of reincarnation. And the main shock: capture. Evil alien civilizations enslaved them millions of years ago. The blood runs cold. Are we puppets in a cosmic prison? Hubbard struck. The new doctrine was given a name: “Scientology.” Scientology. The translation hits like a hammer: “the science of knowing.” 1953. The next step. Hubbard registers the Church of Scientology. Formally—a religious organization. In reality? Tax benefits in the U.S. The perfect cover. Money started flowing in rivers. Decades passed. What began as a doctrine grew into something monstrous. An international corporation. Thousands of employees. Tens of thousands of full-time workers. Its own fleet. Its own “educational centers.” Control over a significant portion of property and capital. An empire built on thetans and tax benefits tightened its grip.

Fromm couldn’t have foreseen this in 1950. Yet as his biographer Rainer Funk would later note when publishing the long-lost review in 2005—after it spent half a century in oblivion—Fromm’s insight proved astonishing. Even then, he recognized in Dianetics what would decades later become one of the world’s most controversial religious corporations.

I won’t analyze Scientology as an organization in depth here—that’s a separate subject worthy of its own voluminous studies. Plenty already exist: I’ll direct readers to works like Lawrence Wright’s Going Clear or Alexander Skorodumov’s Anti-Scientology: A Critique of Hubbardism’s Doctrinal Foundations and Technologies. But consider these titles for a moment. Lawsuits. Criminal cases. Internal scandals. Defectors. Shattered families. Financial fraud across multiple countries. What does an empire woven from such facts conceal? Courts have the answer. France and Belgium ruled it a criminal enterprise at the judicial level. Germany denies it religious status. In 2015, Russia liquidated the central “Church of Scientology Moscow” by order of the Moscow City Court.

But our focus isn’t there. What matters is how Scientology and Dianetics occupy a specific niche within the pandemic of madness—and why understanding that niche is crucial.

Hubbard’s niche operates differently than Hellinger’s or Vianna Stibal’s.

Hellinger and Stibal deal in individual services. Clients pay for sessions, attend, receive them, leave. The relationship ends—or resumes if they return.

Hubbard built something else entirely: a totalizing machine. A system that consumes devotees’ lives step by step until nothing remains outside it. How does it begin? With deceptive simplicity. With cheap bait. A free “personality test” at any center—gentle, harmless, like a hand resting lightly on your shoulder. The first course, “Diagnosis,” costs a pittance. A coin drops into the slot; the mechanism clicks softly. Then comes the “Communication” course. Step. Another step. The person’s inside now. Next—advanced levels. “The Bridge.” The Bridge to Total Freedom. Each rung pricier than the last. The scent of others’ money no longer repels. Each demands more time. The pulse adapts to its new rhythm. Each step—another tether cut. Family. Friends. Career. External life thins, withers, sloughs away like dead skin. Meanwhile, the invisible thread pulls tighter. And binds. Irrevocably. To the organization.

At a certain level, devotees learn the “secret doctrine”—the myth of Xenu, an evil galactic dictator who, 75 million years ago, brought billions of alien souls to Earth, incinerated them in volcanic eruptions, and left their scattered “thetans” clinging to living humans as the source of all misery. Until journalists exposed it in the 1980s, believers only accessed this doctrine after years and tens of thousands spent on the “Bridge.” Scientologists deny its existence; court records from numerous lawsuits prove otherwise.

For our topic, this is crucial: Dianetics is the foundation of this pyramid. It is where the journey of the adept begins. And it is Fromm’s review that marks the first serious academic condemnation of this foundation—a review written three years before Scientology itself emerged as an organization.

Now, why is all of this important for our book on the pandemic of madness?

Because Hubbard illustrates yet another mechanism employed by modern pseudoscience in the realm of mental health.

The first mechanism is mystical. Hellinger and Stibal use it. The client is told: “You have a generational program / a blocking belief in your DNA, and we’ll work through it via a connection to the field / the Creator.” Everything points to invisible forces, untestable and unverifiable.

Here’s the second mechanism. Pseudoscience. Hubbard is its primary operator. Notice: he almost never uses the word “spirit.” He doesn’t talk about “energy.” His vocabulary is different. “Engineering science.” “Reactive mind.” “Analytical mind.” “Engrams.” And the punchline—“the sole source of all neuroses.” What is this? Language. But what kind? Mimicry. A mimicry of science. Of technicality. Of modernity. And here’s the detail. The device. The “E-meter.” A handle. A case. Resembling a primitive galvanometer. Presented as a scientific instrument. And what does it do? “Measures engrams.” Think about that. Measures. Engrams. One question: Where’s the evidence?

The difference between mysticism and pseudoscience lies in their aim—their target audience. Mysticism attracts those whose minds are already open to “spiritual” language, those ready to believe. Pseudoscience aims more precisely. Its target is rational individuals, those who rely on logic but lack the training to distinguish real science from its imitation. They turn away from the “spiritual,” scoff at it, and are therefore utterly defenseless against pseudoscientific language. Engineers, financiers, programmers—they are the victims of this category of fraud. A family constellation practitioner? “It’s all superstition,” the engineer will sneer. They won’t go there. But a Scientology center? “It seems like science,” they’ll nod. And step right into the trap.

The third mechanism—we’ll see it in the next chapter—is the authority of social media. There, neither mysticism nor pseudoscience is needed. Charisma and a large following are enough.

All three mechanisms operate on the same foundation: the gaps left by scientific psychiatry—the lack of quality, accessible care, the long waits to see a doctor, distrust of “pills,” fear of diagnosis, and the natural desire for a quick, simple answer to a complex problem.

And here’s another crucial thought that must be voiced outright.

Hubbard, like Hellinger and Stibal, didn’t emerge from nowhere. He arose as a response to a real problem. That problem was the weakness and limitations of scientific psychiatry in his era.

1950. Dianetics is already written. And psychiatry lies in ruins. Hospitals are overflowing. Not with the wounded. Not with acute cases, but with chronic ones. People who don’t leave—because there’s nowhere to send them. They just lie there. For years. Lobotomy? A scientifically recognized treatment. In just a few months, Egas Moniz will receive a Nobel Prize for it. In medicine. For slicing up brains. Insulin comas. Standard procedure. A patient is put into a coma—deliberately, for hours—to snap them out of psychosis. Or fail to. Electroshock. Without anesthesia. The body contorts in an arch. Teeth shatter. This is treatment. And the first antipsychotic—chlorpromazine—hasn’t even been synthesized yet. It’s still two years away. Psychotherapy? Sure. Psychoanalysis. Long. Expensive. With unclear effectiveness. This was the pit it all grew from. This was the alternative on the table.

A person suffering from anxiety, depression, family issues, or psychosomatic problems would go to a psychiatrist—and receive either nothing at all or a lobotomy. There were almost no alternatives.

Into this void stepped Hubbard. He declared: “Come to me. I am science. I cure everything. Quickly. Reliably. Cheaply (at first).” And thousands followed. Not because they were foolish—but because real medicine had nothing to offer them.

This is an important lesson. The pandemic of madness thrives not only on the despair of its victims and the greed of healers. It feeds on a real vacuum—a black hole left behind by scientific medicine: underdeveloped, underfunded, poorly organized, ethically flawed. Who fans these flames? Every psychiatrist who has given up on a patient. Every healthcare system where people languish for months waiting to see a specialist. Every government that turns a blind eye to mental health programs. Every school that remains silent about mental hygiene. Each one of them is complicit.

Our response is not merely to expose fraudsters. Our response is to build genuine psychiatry that is accessible, scientific, ethical, and timely. Only then will the vacuum cease to form. Only then will the market for the countless offerings of “manufactured happiness” vanish.

Erich Fromm lived another thirty years after that review. He died in Switzerland in 1980. He witnessed the rise of Scientology but did not live to see its decline—though that downfall has yet to come. His warning from 1950 remains relevant today. And it will stay relevant as long as humans crave a quick and easy answer to the question of how to be happy.

There is no answer. There is only a path. And this path is not what Hubbard, Stibal, Hellinger, regression therapists, or their thousands of modern imitators offer.

The Path is evidence-based medicine, evidence-based psychotherapy, honest self-reflection, and acknowledging that our inner lives are complex and resistant to engineering optimization. It may sound less flashy than “pushing buttons”—but it actually works.

RED FLAG. If a method requires “levels,” “stages,” and “initiations,” each more expensive than the last; if the full picture of the teaching is revealed only to the “advanced” practitioners; if those who leave are declared enemies—what you’re facing isn’t a school or therapy. It’s a funnel. You either escape funnels immediately or not at all.

27 / 36

Chapter 23. Inside the Profession Itself: When There Is No Quality Control

So far in this section, I’ve discussed external threats to scientific psychiatry: pre-scientific practices industrialized into a business, and pseudo-medical movements growing alongside proper medicine.

In this chapter, I must address an internal threat. A danger emerging from within our own profession. This is the most painful part of the book for me to write. Because here I’m not discussing outsiders—not the Hellingers or Stibals—but my own colleagues. People who trained alongside me in residency. Those who taught me. Those who officially hold the same diplomas and positions as I do.

Yet I cannot remain silent. Because ignoring the problem is one of the main reasons it persists. And because this pandemic of madness thrives not only due to external fraudsters but also because of our profession’s internal weaknesses.

I’ll present three cases. All come from my own practice. Details have been altered to preserve anonymity. The essence remains unchanged.

Case One. Prayer Instead of an Antipsychotic

2001–2003. I was doing my clinical residency at the V.M. Bekhterev St. Petersburg Psychoneurological Research Institute—one of the oldest institutions of its kind, among the most authoritative academic psychiatry centers across the post-Soviet space. Within these walls lies a mystery: How has this institute endured? The answer lies in its founding date: 1907. Its founder, Vladimir Mikhailovich Bekhterev, was one of the world’s leading neuroanatomists and psychiatrists—a man who peered into the deepest recesses of the brain. His legacy? Today it’s a national medical research center with numerous scientific departments, publications in international journals, and recognition among specialists. Here, the secrets of the mind are kept.

Second Department. The door closed behind me, and I found myself in the domain of Professor Viktor Davidovich Vida—a name spoken with respect throughout the post-Soviet world. One of the most renowned psychiatrists and psychoanalytically oriented psychotherapists. But why him? What lay behind that reputation? Viktor Davidovich was a man of immense culture, vast clinical experience, and international acclaim. His books on schizophrenia psychotherapy and psychodynamics became foundational texts for an entire generation of Russian-speaking psychiatrists. Every page holds a cipher. Every theory—a key to minds slipping into the abyss. To study under him was a privilege. One I still value to this day.

Yet in that same department worked another colleague—let’s call him Dr. S. An older man who’d been at the institute for many years. By diploma—a psychiatrist. An experienced clinician. Respected by patients for his attentiveness and gentle manner.

Dr. S. was a deeply religious Christian. That in itself would be unremarkable—every person’s private faith is their own right. The problem emerged in how Dr. S. integrated his beliefs with clinical practice.

He wrote prayers. With his own hand. On a sheet of paper. And handed them to the patient — with instructions: read this many times a day. Not as a spiritual practice. Not as a supplement to treatment — with the consent of a person who understands: this is my faith, this is not a pill. No. This is what should have raised alarm. This is where it all began. Dr. S. — not a priest, not a faith healer, not a charlatan off the street. A doctor. With a diploma. With a medical chart in his hands. And he wrote the prayer into that chart. As a prescription. As a dosage. As a therapeutic tool. Line by line — in the place where milligrams and dosing frequency belong. His own phrasing sounds like a verdict on evidence-based medicine as a whole: — I see the effect. I see the difference. When a patient prays — they get better.

This happened at one of the leading academic institutions. In 2002. In the schizophrenia ward. With patients diagnosed with psychotic disorders.

I was a young resident. I didn’t know what to do about it. I watched and said nothing.

He didn’t violate a single clause. Not one. Open up post-Soviet ethics codes — not a word about prohibiting prayer. Not a line about banning “prayer therapy.” Nothing. Full stop. Twenty-five years of difference. A quarter century of clinical experience against my theory. His intuition — wiser. That’s how it was supposed to be. That’s what’s carved into the hindbrain of anyone who ever saw him at a patient’s bedside: these hands, remembering more than any book. And what if it works? What if patients really do leave the ward different — through placebo, through supportive engagement, through whatever — what then? Where’s the error? Where’s the violation? There’s the question. There’s the puzzle. Three dry “technically”s — and not a single answer.

The problem is this.

When a psychiatrist places prayer on the same footing as an antipsychotic, he undermines the very status of scientific medicine. He tells the patient: “Taking haloperidol is one intervention, and prayer is another. Both work.” The patient receives the message: science and religious practice have equal therapeutic status. And from there unfolds the logic the patient inevitably develops on his own: if prayer helps — maybe it’s enough on its own? If I pray fervently — maybe I won’t need the medication?

We are talking about patients with schizophrenia. About people who, if you don’t treat them with antipsychotics in the acute phase, face progressive deterioration, social maladjustment, loss of contact with reality, and s••••e. Any message that weakens their connection to evidence-based pharmacotherapy is a message that ultimately leads to death.

But there was something worse. Dr. S. was being watched by young colleagues: interns, residents, graduate students. For them, Bekhterev was becoming their first real clinical experience. The price of mistakes here was someone else’s psyche. And here they saw it: a professorial colleague practicing “prayer therapy” right in the clinic. No one stopped him. No one grabbed his hand. What does that mean to a novice? Is this normal? Is it normal — to mix scientific medicine with spiritual practices? Is it normal — to consider your own subjective religious intuition equivalent to neuroscience? Decades of clinical research? Burn it. Throw it out. Ignore it. Because the main thing is to “see the effect” with your own eyes.

I believe that Dr. S. was replicating the structure of a pandemic of madness within its very adversary, within academic psychiatry. This is a form of internal betrayal of scientific medicine. Not malicious—he was a kind man. But no less harmful because of it.

And the most alarming part isn’t that there was one such doctor. It’s that there were many—in Bekhterev’s clinic and in other institutions across the post-Soviet space. Some wrote prayers, some recommended “visiting a priest,” some applied “bioenergetic” methods, some chanted mantras to patients, and some referred them to “specialists in ancestral memory” (!). None of this was an exception. It happened within the bounds of what was considered normal by a segment of post-Soviet psychiatry.

That’s why I consider the topic of this chapter no less important than the issue of pseudomystical healers on social media. If there’s a leak within the very citadel of scientific psychiatry, no fight against pseudoscience outside its walls will help.

Case Two. Thirteen Years of OCD and “Sex Therapy”

A thirty-three-year-old woman came to see me. Let’s call her Leila. She had a higher education, a steady job, and financial independence. She had never been married. She came from a religious family but wasn’t strictly observant herself.

Her problems began at sixteen. Obsessive-compulsive disorder. Compulsive fears of contamination. Hours spent washing her hands. Compulsively checking whether the stove was off or the doors were locked. Exhausting obsessive thoughts she couldn’t shake. Constant anxiety. Sleep—four to five hours a night. A feeling that her life was spent in a battle with her own brain.

Thirteen years before we met—from sixteen to twenty-nine—Leila sought help from specialists. First, psychiatrists in her hometown. Then, in Moscow. Then, in Istanbul, where her parents took her to see “the best doctor.”

Thirteen years. A full thirteen years—and what did she get in return? Almost nothing. Just a handful of pills. The first psychiatrist. Fluoxetine. A low dose. She felt better for six months. Then everything returned. The darkness closed in again. The second. Paroxetine. A replacement. The third added quetiapine. For the anxiety. The fourth prescribed clonazepam. To sleep. The fifth—risperidone. Low doses. By the time she was twenty-eight, Leila was taking four medications at once: an antidepressant, an atypical antipsychotic, a benzodiazepine, and a sleeping pill. Four chemical keys, each locking a different cage in her exhausted body. Every one of these drugs had been prescribed to her at some point. Each helped for a short time. Each stopped working—or caused side effects. And then it was ruthlessly swapped for the next.

Not one of these psychiatrists offered her psychotherapy.

It would have been shocking—if it weren’t, alas, so typical.

International guidelines for treating OCD—issued by the American Psychiatric Association, NICE (UK), and the World Health Organization—agree on one thing: the first-line therapy for OCD is exposure and response prevention (ERP), a specialized form of cognitive-behavioral therapy. The effectiveness of this therapy is approximately 80% in terms of significant improvement for patients who receive it correctly. Medications can complement the therapy; for moderate severity, SSRI monotherapy without CBT may sometimes suffice; but treating OCD solely with medication, without psychotherapy, violates the standard of care.

So why, over thirteen years, did Leila never receive psychotherapy?

The reasons intertwine into a suffocating bouquet. The first—across the post-Soviet region, genuine CBT with ERP is virtually inaccessible. There are only a handful of specialists in each major city who have undergone proper training and practice according to global standards. What do the rest call “cognitive-behavioral therapy”? Anything and everything—from generic advice like “think positively” to outright eclecticism flavored with esotericism. A counterfeit instead of salvation. The second reason is even more glaring: psychiatrists themselves often aren’t aware of the actual effectiveness of ERP. They’re firmly locked into the biomedical paradigm, where the only answer is to prescribe a pill. Write a prescription. Suppress the symptom. The third reason delivers a gut punch—pure economics. A psychiatrist’s appointment is covered by insurance. A psychotherapist’s? Not at all. Or a pittance. A psychiatrist sees a patient for fifteen minutes. A psychotherapist must work for an hour. Weekly. For several long months. Economics ruthlessly pushes for a pharmacological solution. The system grinds people down in the mills of pills.

For thirteen years, Leila went along. Took the medications. Waited. “Maybe this drug will work.” It didn’t. Or it worked for two or three months, then faded.

And then—here begins the heaviest part of her story—one of her treating psychiatrists, seeing that the medications weren’t helping, decided to suggest something else. In this doctor’s words, it was called “sex therapy.”

The essence of the proposal was this: “You’re in your thirties. You’ve never had sexual relations. Freud showed that most neuroses are tied to repressed sexuality. If you engage in sexual relations—your OCD will go away.” The psychiatrist then offered himself as a partner—“not as entertainment, but specifically as part of the therapeutic process.” He explained that this was precisely why “sex therapy” should be conducted by a trusted professional, not a random individual.

Leila agreed.

Let’s pause. Why did she agree?

She wasn’t foolish. She wasn’t frivolous. She was worn down by thirteen years of futile treatment. She trusted the doctor—this wasn’t her first psychiatrist, and she had been working with this particular one for several months. She was desperate. She was offered a last-ditch solution by someone she considered a professional. Her ethnic and religious beliefs were firmly against premarital relations, but her desperate need to escape her illness outweighed them. She agreed, overcoming her inner resistance, the way one consents to surgery.
